```yaml
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: frame_depth
feature_id: feature_candidate_325
feature_label: reported_dialogue_frame
observation: "Socrates opens by referring to a previous day's discussion, summarizing its main topic ('head' / kephalaion) and asking Timaeus to confirm that it was spoken to everyone's satisfaction."
textual_basis: "At 17c, Socrates says 'yesterday somewhere of the speeches spoken by me concerning the constitution the head was what sort and from what sort of men the best would appear to me to come to be,' and Timaeus confirms that it was spoken to the satisfaction of all."
limits: "This observation records the frame reference; it does not adjudicate whether the referenced discussion is the Republic or a distinct earlier conversation."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1574
feature_label: recapitulation_or_restart
observation: "Socrates recapitulates parts of the previous day's city-foundation argument: the separation of farmers and other crafts from the warrior class, the assignment of one craft per person according to nature, and the description of guardians as sole defenders of the city."
textual_basis: "At 17c-17d, Socrates asks whether they first separated the class of farmers and other crafts from the race of those who would fight on behalf of the city, and then asserts that by giving each one pursuit according to nature, one craft each, they said the guardians must be only defenders of the city, judging gently those within."
limits: "This observation records recapitulation as a dramatic device; it does not claim the prior discussion is definitively the Republic, nor does it assess the truth of the recapitulated claims."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: guardian_selection_profile
observation: "Socrates recapitulates the guardian nature requirement from the Republic: the guardians' soul must be both spirited (thumoeides) and philosophic (philosophon) in a distinguished way."
textual_basis: "At 18a Socrates says they described a certain nature of the guardians' soul that must be at once thumoeides and at once philosophon in a distinguished way. Timaeus confirms with nai."
limits: "This is a recapitulation of a prior logos, not a new argument. The observation records that the dual soul-nature profile is repeated; it does not assess whether the Timaeus version differs from Republic details."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1139
feature_label: guardian_education_legislation_program
observation: "Socrates recapitulates the guardian education program: upbringing in gymnastic and music and whatever studies belong to these, and the prohibition of private property with communal living."
textual_basis: "At 18a-b Socrates asks whether the guardians were not to be raised in gymnastic and music and whatever studies pertain to them, and then recounts that those so raised should hold no gold, silver, or other private possession but receive a measured wage as auxiliaries, spend in common, live together, and have care for virtue continuously while keeping leisure from other pursuits. Timaeus confirms at 18b that these things were said in that way."
limits: "This is a summary recollection, not a new legislative act within the Timaeus. The observation notes the recapitulation; it does not analyze differences from the Republic account."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 18c Socrates explicitly recalls these provisions using phrases that mark them as already agreed, for example 'we mentioned concerning women.' Socrates uses the term 'easy to remember' (18c and 18d) and asks 'do we remember' (18d). Timaeus confirms at 18c that these things were spoken in that way and at 18d affirms they are easy to remember."
limits: "The recapitulation covers only the two provisions about women and procreation. It does not summarize every detail of the previous day's city-in-speech discussion."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0013

greek_terms:
  - κλήροις
  - τύχην ἡγουμένοις αἰτίαν
english_gloss: "by lots...considering chance the cause of the pairing"
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_1644
feature_label: luck_as_masking_device
observation: "At 18e, Socrates describes using a lottery device to pair good and bad citizens so that each pair with similar natures, and adds that they should attribute the outcome to chance to avoid enmity. The explicit rationale is that the rulers manipulate the lottery so that citizens believe chance, not design, governs the pairing."
textual_basis: "Socrates says the rulers must contrive by lots so that the bad separately and the good will each pair with similar natures, and adds lest any enmity arise among them through these things, believing chance to be the cause of the pairing (18e-19a). The verb for contrive indicates deliberate design; the citizens are to regard the lottery outcome as chance."
limits: "This observation notes the explicit design of a masking device in the reported ideal city. It does not claim that this reflects Socrates' or Plato's own view of the dialogue's structure, nor that the dialogue itself employs an esoteric masking strategy."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates disavows his own ability to praise the men and the city adequately, then generalizes the difficulty to poets whose imitation is shaped by their upbringing."
textual_basis: "At 19d Socrates says he has judged himself unable to become capable of adequately praising the men and city. He then says this is not surprising in his own case and extends the judgment to poets, because the mimetic tribe most easily and best imitates what it has been reared among."
limits: "This records an encomiastic capacity disavowal, not a claim that Socrates lacks knowledge in general. The continuation develops the contrast with sophists and the interlocutors' qualifications."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "The analogy at 19b contrasts animals produced by painting (hypo graphes eirgasmena) with truly living ones (zonta alethinos) and then pivots to the desire to see them moving and competing (kinoumena ... kata ten agonian athlounta). Socrates applies the same feeling (tauton kai ego pepontha) to the city described earlier."
limits: "This is a single analogy used as a dramatic transition, not a developed craft analogy in the argumentative sense. It does not here develop into a theory of mimesis."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

feature_label: initial_question_stated
observation: "Socrates asks Critias what the story (logos) was. Critias responds that it concerned a deed 'of the greatest and most renowned and most justly so of all deeds' that Athens once performed but whose account has not survived to the present time due to time and the destruction of those who performed it. Socrates then asks Critias to relate it from the beginning."
textual_basis: "When Socrates asks 'what was the story, Critias?' (21d), Critias replies that it was 'about the greatest and most renowned of all, most justly so, deed — which this city once performed but the account has not endured to the present time due to time and the destruction of those who performed it' (21d). Socrates then asks him to tell it from the beginning (21d). This sets the dialogue's dramatic subject as the recovery of a lost Athenian deed."
limits: "This observation records the dramatic program being set. It does not address whether the account is fictitious or the historical import of the claim. The content of the deed is not yet specified in this segment."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1611
feature_label: reported_divine_source_frame
observation: "Critias locates the origin of the story in the Egyptian district of Sais, where the goddess worshipped is Neith in Egyptian but identified as Athena in Greek according to the Egyptians' own account. The people of Sais are described as particularly friendly to Athens (philathenaioi) and claim a kinship with Athenians."
textual_basis: "Critias specifies at 21e: the Saitic nome in the Delta where the Nile divides, with Sais as its greatest city — home of King Amasis — and the city's founding goddess Neith, called Athena in Greek by the account of the Egyptians, who are 'greatly friendly to Athenians and claim to be in some way akin to them.' The cultural syncretism between the Egyptian goddess Neith and the Greek Athena is explicitly marked."
limits: "This observation records the prosopographic and mythic-geographic framing. It does not assess the historical validity of the Egyptian-Athenian kinship claim or the goddess identification. The account of the deed itself has not yet been delivered."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_1467
feature_label: myth_catalog_enumerated
observation: The Egyptian priest reports that Solon, when prompted to speak about ancient matters, attempted to recount the oldest Greek traditions by mythologizing about Phoroneus (called the first), Niobe, and after the flood about Deucalion and Pyrrha, then genealogized their descendants and tried to count the years.
textual_basis: At 22a-b, Solon is described as attempting to speak of Phoroneus the first-named, Niobe, and after the cataclysm Deucalion and Pyrrha, mythologizing about how they survived, genealogizing their descendants, and attempting to compute the chronology by number of years.
limits: This record identifies the enumeration of mythic figures as a narrative move within a reported dialogue; it makes no claim about the historical truth of these figures or about Plato's own view of Greek myth.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The Egyptian priest claims that the finest and best race once existed among humans in the land of Athens (23b), and that Solon and the whole present city descend from a small seed that survived repeated destructions (23b-23c)."
textual_basis: "The priest explicitly states the Athenians do not know about this race (23b) and attributes their ignorance to the survivors dying without writing for many generations (23c)."
limits: "This observation records the aetiological claim made by a character within the dialogue. It does not assess the historical truth of this claim or whether Plato endorses it."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The speaker enumerates the distinct castes or occupational classes of the ancient Athenian polis, separated from one another by law: priests, craftsmen, herdsmen, hunters, farmers, and the warrior class.
textual_basis: At 24a-24b the Egyptian priest lists occupations in sequence (priests, craftsmen, herdsmen, hunters, farmers, then the warrior class) describing each as set apart from the others by law, directly echoing the caste separation program of the Republic's city-in-speech.
limits: This observation notes the caste catalogue as a textual fact; it does not assert that Timaeus endorses or critiques the arrangement, nor does it claim this passage is the sole or original version of the city-foundation motif in the Platonic corpus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1997
feature_label: geographic_marker_links_myth_to_empirical_world
observation: Critias anchors the Atlantis narrative to a known empirical reference point by naming the Pillars of Heracles as the mouth of the strait beyond which the Atlantic island lay.
textual_basis: At 24e Critias reports that a great island lay before the mouth which the Greeks call the Pillars of Heracles. The parenthetical address to the interlocutors (hos phate, hymeis, "as you say, you") marks this as a shared contemporary landmark, linking the mythic geography to the actual Mediterranean exit.
limits: This observation identifies the textual device of anchoring myth in known geography. It does not address whether Plato believed the story, nor its truth-value.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἀληθινὸν
  - πέλαγος ὄντως
  - ἀληθῶς
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_1998
feature_label: reality_hierarchy_by_contrastive_negation
observation: Critias constructs a reality hierarchy by contrasting the Mediterranean with the Atlantic: what lies within the strait is a mere harbor with a narrow entrance, while the ocean beyond is truly sea and the land surrounding it is wholly truly continent.
textual_basis: At 25a Critias says what is inside the mouth appears to be a harbor having a narrow entrance, while that sea beyond is truly sea (pelagos ontos) and the land enclosing it would be most correctly called wholly truly continent (pantelos alethos ... epeiros). The triple insistence on truth/being words marks a reality contrast between the familiar and the realm of the story.
limits: This observation identifies a contrastive reality-marking device within the mythic frame. It does not claim that Plato endorses or subverts the reality claims made in the passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The Atlantis island is introduced as the site of a great and wondrous coalition of kings whose dominion extended beyond the island itself to many other islands and parts of the continent, using geographic scale claims to establish the magnitude of the mythic power.
textual_basis: At 25a Critias states that in this island of Atlantis a great and wondrous power of kings was established, ruling the whole island, many other islands, and parts of the continent. The combination of size language (megale) and wonder language (thaumaste) with geographic sweep (the whole island plus many other islands and continental parts) constructs the empire's scale as a mythic datum.
limits: This observation identifies a narrative device for establishing mythic magnitude. It does not address the historicity of Atlantis or the political valence of the empire description.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 24c the text says the goddess "selected the place in which you were born, having observed the good temperament of the seasons in it, because it would bear most prudent men." This is a causal link from climate to human character.
limits: This observation records a climate-to-character causal claim within the mythic narrative; it does not assert that the dialogue endorses environmental determinism as a doctrine or that this passage establishes a systematic theory.
```

```yaml
observation: |
  The Egyptian priest at Sais disclaims any envy before narrating ancient Athenian history to Solon, then gives three positive motives: for Solon's sake, for Athens's sake, and especially for the goddess who founded both cities.
textual_basis: |
  At 23d the priest prefaces his account with a denial of phthonos and follows it with three stated reasons for speaking (for Solon, for Athens, for the goddess). This turns the embedded report of the Atlantis story into an act of deliberate goodwill rather than a grudging or competitive disclosure.
limits: |
  Does not establish that the priest is reliable or that the Atlantis narrative is factual; only records the framing device.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0055
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 26a-26b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 26a-26b

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: frame_depth
feature_id: feature_candidate_151
feature_label: narrator_memory_disclaimer
observation: "The speaker (Critias) reports that he could not immediately recount the story from the previous day because he had not sufficiently remembered it after a lapse of time (dia chronou gar ouch hikanos ememnemen, 26a). He then describes a process of first rehearsing everything adequately to himself (pros emauton proton hikanos panta analabonta, 26a) and subsequently reconstructing the account overnight (apelthon te schedon ti panta episkopon tes nuktos anelabon, 26b). He remarks that what he heard only yesterday he may not be able to recall entirely, but astonishes that anything he heard long ago (pampolyn chronon, 26b) could have escaped him."
textual_basis: "At 26a Critias explains he did not speak immediately because he could not sufficiently remember after a lapse of time; at 26a-26b he narrates the process of private rehearsal then overnight reconstruction; at 26b he explicitly contrasts his unreliable recent memory with his reliable remote memory."
limits: "This observation records a memory disclaimer within the frame narrative; it does not assess whether Critias is truthful or whether this hedge signals unreliability of the embedded Atlantis account."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 26a-26b Critias describes a temporal sequence: yesterday's agreement, immediate departure and rehearsal with companions, overnight review. This establishes a layered narrative frame where the present speech is the product of a remembered and reconstructed prior agreement."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 27a, Critias describes Timaeus as astronomikotaton (most skilled in astronomy) and as having made it his work to know the nature of the whole, and prescribes that he speak first, beginning from the generation of the cosmos."
limits: "This observation notes the character profile and speech assignment without assessing whether the subsequent discourse reflects astronomical expertise or whether the identification is accurate."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0072

  end_char: 21554
  text_sha256: d8618985eb113fe76d2f105a0ee092af6757f8b5d9c99c910dc870fcd959691a
greek_terms: ["τὸ ὂν ἀεί, γένεσιν δὲ οὐκ ἔχον", "τὸ γιγνόμενον μὲν ἀεί, ὂν δὲ οὐδέποτε", "νοήσει μετὰ λόγου", "δόξῃ μετ' αἰσθήσεως ἀλόγου"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_2265
feature_label: being_becoming_distinction_with_epistemic_correlates
observation: "At 27d-28a, Timaeus introduces a foundational ontological distinction between what always is and has no becoming, and what is always becoming but never is, each paired with a distinct mode of cognition: the former is grasped by intellection with reason, the latter by opinion with irrational sense-perception."
textual_basis: "Timaeus at 27d-28a divides all things into (1) what is always, having no becoming, grasped by intellection with reason (noesis meta logou), always being in the same respect; and (2) what is always becoming, never being, grasped by opinion with irrational sense-perception (doxa met' aistheseos alogou), coming to be and perishing, never really being."
limits: "This observation records the distinction as stated. It does not claim that this is a theory of Forms identical to that in the Phaedo or Republic, nor does it assess whether this two-level ontology is consistently maintained throughout the Timaeus."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: ["κατὰ λόγον τὸν εἰκότα"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_2272
feature_label: likely_account_hedge
observation: The speaker explicitly marks the account as a "likely account" (eikōs logos) at 30b-c, signaling that what follows is not demonstrative truth but the most probable account available.
textual_basis: At the close of the reasoning about the cosmos as an ensouled intelligent living creature (30b-c), the speaker inserts the formula "according to the likely account" (kata logon ton eikota) — thereby framing the whole preceding construction (intellect in soul, soul in body) as an eikōs logos rather than a necessary demonstration.
limits: This observation does not assess whether other passages in Timaeus use similar hedges, nor does it determine the epistemological status of the likely account in relation to the forms. It only notes the explicit demarcation formula.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_2273
feature_label: intelligible_paradigm_for_cosmos
observation: At 30c, the speaker identifies the model for the cosmos as the intelligible living creature which contains all other intelligible living things within itself, rejecting as models any particular living creatures that exist in the form of a part.
textual_basis: At 30c, the speaker asks which living creature the cosmos was made in likeness of, rejects particular creatures (those existing in the form of a part) because nothing incomplete can be beautiful, and posits instead that the cosmos is most like that creature of which the other living things are parts, individually and by kinds — identified as the intelligible living creature which encompasses all intelligible living things in itself.
limits: This observation does not evaluate whether this intelligible paradigm is a Platonic Form in the strict sense, nor does it compare this model with the Form of the Good or other Forms in other dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  text_sha256: 227fb309184e71df7ec652a9c144c644dd5d3b8f4f8b7dc8e2d55a35da1f676b
greek_terms: ["δεσμὸν", "δεσμῶν", "ἀναλογία"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: mathematical_cosmology
feature_id: feature_candidate_2459
feature_label: two_bound_by_third_proportion
observation: "Timaeus states a mathematical principle that two things alone cannot be well combined without a third, that a bond must come between them. The finest bond is that which makes itself and the bound things maximally one, and this is achieved by proportion."
textual_basis: "At 31c, the text says two alone cannot be well combined without a third. It proceeds to identify the finest bond as that which makes unity, and this is naturally accomplished by proportion."
limits: "This records the geometric bonding principle. It does not assess whether the reasoning applies the principle correctly to cosmic composition, nor does it resolve what the third term will be (this unfolds in later passages)."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 32d-33a, three purpose clauses are marked by 'first, so that...' and 'further, so that...': (1) that the cosmos be a whole complete living being from complete parts, (2) that it be one since nothing was left out from which another such thing could arise, (3) that it be ageless and without sickness. A causal explanation follows at 33a: external forces dissolve composite bodies and bring disease and old age."
limits: "This observation notes the syntactic structure of the purpose chain; it does not evaluate whether the teleological reasoning is valid or whether it reflects Plato's own view rather than Timaeus's character speech."
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - μεσότης
  - ἓν
  - ἐξ ἀνάγκης
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: cosmogonic_proportion
feature_id: feature_candidate_2526
feature_label: proportion_yields_unity
observation: The text presents a formal mathematical principle that when a middle term stands in continuous proportion to extremes, all terms collapse into identity and unity, and this principle is then applied to the binding of the cosmic body through elemental means.
textual_basis: At 32a the text states that if the middle is such that the first is to it as it is to the last, then the middle becomes first and last and the extremes become middles, so all are necessarily the same and all will be one. At 32b this proportion principle is applied when the god places water and air as means between fire and earth and works out their proportional ratios, binding the cosmos into a unified whole.
limits: This observation records the cosmogonic use of mathematical proportion. It does not evaluate the mathematical validity of the argument or claim that Timaeus invented the proportion principle.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - στερεά
  - μεσότητες
  - ἐπίπεδον
  - βάθος
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: cosmogonic_proportion
feature_id: feature_candidate_2527
feature_label: solid_requires_two_means
observation: The text argues that the solidity of the cosmic body necessitates two intermediate elements rather than one, because solids are always bound by two means whereas a plane body would need only one mean. This geometric constraint directly determines the number of elements in the cosmos.
textual_basis: At 32a-32b the text presents a conditional: if the body of the universe needed to be a plane without depth, one mean would suffice to bind it; but since it must be solid, and solids are always fitted together by two means, never one, two means are required. This geometric reasoning explains why four elements (fire, earth, water, air) are needed rather than three.
limits: This observation records the geometric constraint argument linking solidity to the number of elemental means. It does not assess whether the geometric claim about solids is mathematically correct or whether it originates with Plato.
review_status: accepted
```

The verbs of placing, working out, binding, and constituting are drawn from the domain of craft and construction.

```yaml
  - σφαιροειδές

feature_family: teleological_reasoning
feature_id: feature_candidate_2638
feature_label: fittingness_premise
observation: The spherical shape is chosen for the cosmos through a fittingness argument: the shape given is the fitting and the akin. Because the cosmos is to contain all living beings within it, the fitting shape is the one that contains all shapes — the sphere. Likeness is judged far better than unlikeness.
textual_basis: At 33b, the fitting-and-akin premise introduces the shape choice. The reasoning chain moves from the cosmos as container of all living beings to the sphere as the shape that contains all shapes, concluding with the spherical designation. The aesthetic-teleological principle that likeness is far better than unlikeness closes the argument.
limits: This observation identifies the structure of the fittingness argument; it does not assess whether the argument is valid, nor does it claim this is the only mode of reasoning in the Timaeus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The cosmos is described as needing nothing external: it lacks eyes, hearing, respiration, and nutritive organs because no external visible, audible, breathable, incoming, or outgoing object remains for them. It nourishes itself from its own decay and is judged better as self-sufficient than as needing others.
textual_basis: At 33c-33d, each denied organ is paired with the absence of an external correlate. The passage adds that nothing departs from or approaches the cosmos, that it provides nourishment to itself from its own decay, and that the maker judged it better as self-sufficient than needing others.
limits: This observation registers the self-sufficiency claim and its negative argument structure. It does not address the prior spherical-shape argument or the later removal of hands and feet.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: "The demiurge constructs a three-tiered ontology of being: indivisible being that is always in the same state, divisible being that comes to be in the realm of bodies, and a third intermediate form of being mixed from both (35a)."
textual_basis: "The text explicitly distinguishes three kinds of being (ousia): (1) the indivisible and always unchanging kind, (2) the divisible kind associated with bodies and becoming, and (3) a third form (triton...ousias eidos) mixed from both, placed in the middle (en mesōi). This is distinct from the later mixing of Same and Different, which are added to the being-mixture."
limits: "This observation records the textual layering of being-types. It does not claim that this is a doctrine of forms or that the intermediate being corresponds to soul alone; the passage itself locates the mixing within the soul's construction."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0094
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 38a-38b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 38a-38b
  start_marker: 38a
  end_marker: 38b
  start_char: 35973
  end_char: 36847
  text_sha256: bea9e5183210b4a11c84c74628950850362f6bf756ec4bfd7bebe7057c60a4d1
greek_terms:
  - ἦν
  - ἔσται
  - αἰών
  - χρόνος
english_gloss: "'was' and 'will be'; eternity vs. time"
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_2858
feature_label: temporal_predicates_denied_of_eternal
observation: |
  At 38a, Timaeus asserts that the temporal predicates 'was' and 'will be' apply only to becoming (genesis) in time, while what is always in the same state without motion (to aei kata tauta echon akinetos) cannot properly be said to become older or younger, to have become, to have come to be now, or to be going to be in the future.
textual_basis: |
  The passage explicitly distinguishes two registers: 'was' and 'will be' are called movements (kineseis) proper to genesis in time, while the eternal unchanging being receives none of the temporal determinations that genesis attaches to things carried in perception. The contrast between eternal being and time-bound becoming is drawn through the denial of temporal predicates to the former.
limits: |
  The passage does not use the term 'form' (eidos/idea) in this denial, nor does it specify participation or separation vocabulary. The observation records a predicate denial pattern that is structurally akin to form-language claims about eternality but does not prove Timaeus is speaking about Platonic forms per se.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_2859
feature_label: time_as_moving_image_of_eternity
observation: |
  Timaeus describes time as having come into being as a moving image of eternity (aionos eikona), remaining according to number (kat' arithmon) while circling. This mimetic relationship between time and eternity is stated at 38a, where time imitates eternity and circles according to number, and again at 38b where time comes into being together with the heaven so that they might be as similar as possible to the eternal paradigm.
textual_basis: |
  At 38a, time is said to imitate eternity and circle according to number. At 38b-38c, time comes into being with the heaven according to the paradigm of everlasting nature so that it may be as similar as possible to it.
limits: |
  This observation records the mimetic relation of time to eternity stated in the text. It does not evaluate whether this image-of-eternity claim is consistent with other Platonic treatments of time, nor does it claim the passage is a form-participation claim rather than a cosmological image claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0096
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 38a-38b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 38a-38b
  start_marker: 38a
  end_marker: 38b
  start_char: 35973
  end_char: 36847
  text_sha256: bea9e5183210b4a11c84c74628950850362f6bf756ec4bfd7bebe7057c60a4d1
greek_terms:
  - διακριβολογεῖσθαι
  - καιρὸς
english_gloss: "to speak precisely; appropriate occasion"
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_2860
feature_label: speaker_defers_precise_discussion
observation: |
  At 38b, after listing the temporal distinctions of 'what has come to be', 'what is coming to be', 'what is going to come to be', and 'what is not being', Timaeus remarks that none of these are spoken with precision (akribes), and that the present moment may not be the fitting occasion (kairos) to speak precisely (diakribologeisthai) about them. He then moves on without pursuing the precise account.
textual_basis: |
  Timaeus says 'none of which we speak precisely' (hon ouden akribes legomen), then adds 'about these matters it might not be a fitting occasion in the present to speak with precision' (peri men oun touton tach' an ouk eie kairos prepon en to paronti diakribologeisthai). The speaker names his own lack of precision and defers the precise treatment, then continues the discourse.
limits: |
  This is a local deferral move at 38b only. It does not establish that the entire dialogue operates under a systematic precision disclaimer, nor does it identify irony. It records a patterned speaker move where precision is acknowledged as absent and deferred, which structurally resembles irony-marker deferrals elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1246
feature_label: demiourgos_production_mapping
observation: The god kindles a light (the sun) in the second orbit from earth so that the heavens would be illuminated as completely as possible and living beings would partake of number by learning from the revolution of the Same and the Similar.
textual_basis: At 39b the text states that the god (ho theos) lit a light in the second of the orbits around earth, which we now call the sun (helion), for two purposes: to shine through the whole heaven (eis hapanta phainoi ton ouranon) and so that living beings (zoa) might share in number (arithmou) by learning from the revolution of the Same and Similar (tes tautou kai homoiou periphoras). This maps the divine craftsman's production (ignition of the sun) onto a target domain of enabling cosmic time-measurement and arithmetical cognition by ensouled beings.
limits: The observation records the craft-like production act and its stated purposes. It does not evaluate the astronomy, analyze the causal mechanism of ignition, or claim that this passage establishes the demiurge-as-craftsman motif on its own.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 39c, Timaeus gives a compact sequence of temporal definitions. Night and day are the period of the one wisest revolution; month occurs when the moon completes its circle and overtakes the sun; year occurs when the sun completes its own circle."
limits: "This records the definitional sequence for temporal units. It does not assess astronomical accuracy, and it does not include the following few-versus-many claim about unnamed planetary periods."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The speaker contrasts the few (oligoi) who have conceived the periods of the other planets with the many (polloi) who have not, and states that the many neither name them nor measure them by numbers.
textual_basis: At 39c the text says that humans, except a few of the many (plen oligoi ton pollon), have not conceived (ouk ennenoekotes) the periods of the other planets and therefore neither name nor symmetrically measure them by numbers. This draws an explicit cognitive/naming divide between a knowledgeable few and the ignorant many.
limits: The observation records the few-versus-many distinction as a prosopographic feature of the passage. It does not identify who the few are, compare this to other dialogues' few/many contrasts, or claim an esoteric teaching.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: >-
  At 37b-37c the text states that understanding and knowledge are produced when the
  soul's circle of the Same grasps the intelligible. If anyone claims that the locus
  where these two arise is anything other than soul, he speaks falsely. The passage
  thus makes soul the exclusive substrate of understanding and knowledge.
textual_basis: >-
  The clause at 37c states that if anyone claims the seat of these two (understanding
  and knowledge) is anything other than soul, he will speak anything but the truth.
  "These two" refers to the two cognitive products just enumerated at 37c. The text
  explicitly bars any identification of their seat with anything other than soul.
limits: >-
  This observation does not claim that the passage defines soul or that the text is
  committed to a specific psychology. It only records the explicit exclusion of any
  non-soul substrate for understanding and knowledge.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 38e-39a, the text describes bodies bound with living bonds, the motion of the Different as oblique (plagian) passing through the motion of the Same which dominates it, with smaller circles moving faster and larger slower; the motion of the Same turns all their circles into a spiral (helika) so that the fastest appear to catch up with and be caught by the slower."
limits: "This records only the kinematic description of planetary motion through the Same/Different framework; it does not assess the astronomical accuracy of the model or whether the passage functions as myth or proto-science."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_3059
feature_label: divine_craft_production
observation: |
  The Demiurge declares that the generated gods, though not intrinsically immortal or indissoluble, will escape death because his will (boulēsis) constitutes a bond stronger and more authoritative than the bonds with which they were put together at their genesis (41b).
  This is a divine address embedded in a cosmological myth.
textual_basis: |
  At 41b the Demiurge speaks directly to the generated gods, stating they are not athanatoi (immortal) nor alutoi (indissoluble) yet will not be dissolved nor meet death's portion, because they have received a greater and more authoritative bond of his will than the bonds used when they were put together.
limits: |
  The observation does not analyze the metaphysical status of the Demiurge's will or evaluate whether this constitutes a genuine immortality guarantee.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  At 41c the Demiurge says there remain three ungenerated mortal kinds, and if they are not produced the heaven will be incomplete. He then commands the gods: "turn according to nature to the demiurgy of living beings, imitating my power concerning your own genesis." This frames the Demiurge as a master craftsman delegating work to apprentice craftsmen.
limits: |
```

```yaml
feature_label: teleological_completeness_requirement
observation: |
  The Demiurge states a teleological requirement for cosmic completion: three mortal kinds remain ungenerated, and without their generation the heaven will be incomplete (atelēs). The heaven must contain all kinds of living beings if it is to be sufficiently complete (teleos hikanōs) (41b-c).
textual_basis: |
  At 41b-c the Demiurge says three mortal kinds remain ungenerated; if these do not come into being, "the heaven will be incomplete" (ouranos atelēs estai), and "it must be, if it is to be sufficiently complete" (dei de, ei mellei teleos hikanōs einai). The cosmos must contain all genera of living things.
limits: |
  The observation does not identify what the three mortal kinds are or evaluate whether the teleological argument succeeds as a philosophical claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 39d-39e, the text states the stars were generated "so that this cosmos would be as similar as possible to the perfect and intelligible Living Creature for the imitation of its everlasting nature," and that the craftsman crafted the remainder "fashioning it in imitation of the nature of the paradigm."
limits: This does not prove that the paradigm is a separately existing Form; it records that paradigm-language serves as a standard for cosmic production within the Timaeus cosmology.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0115
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 42c-42d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 42c-42d
  start_marker: 42c
  end_marker: 42d
  start_char: 45132
  end_char: 45878
  text_sha256: "255991bc3b701225faca13584d8ca922b91163bd17d32e10151623b5140d11db"
greek_terms:
  - δευτέρᾳ γενέσει
  - μεταβολοῖ
  - θήρειον φύσιν
  - λόγῳ κρατήσας
  - εἶδος ἕξεως
  - διαθεσμοθετήσας
english_gloss: "second birth, changes, beastly nature, mastered by reason, form of condition, legislated"
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_3081
feature_label: psychic_reincarnation_sequence
observation: "The demiurge legislates a reincarnation sequence: a soul that persists in vice undergoes transformation into progressively lower beastly natures (42c), and only by overcoming the irrational accretions from fire, water, air, and earth through reason can it return to its original best form of condition (42c-42d)."
textual_basis: "At 42c the text links a second birth to moral failure, names a 'beastly nature' as the terminus of repeated transformation, and specifies rational mastery as the means to regain the first and best 'form of condition'. The demiurge's legislative act brackets this as a cosmic law."
limits: "This observation identifies a bounded mythic account of soul transmigration as a textual phenomenon. It does not claim that the passage is esoteric, that it encodes a hidden doctrine, or that it represents a specific historical Pythagorean influence."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  start_char: 45480
  end_char: 46358
  text_sha256: 2afdbf34d06c559e65dbfb441a758c4566121ad70b3b114631c10216285d7870
greek_terms:
  - ἔσπειρεν
  - τοὺς μὲν εἰς γῆν, τοὺς δ’ εἰς σελήνην, τοὺς δ’ εἰς τἆλλα
  - ὄργανα χρόνου
  - σώματα πλάττειν θνητά
  - ψυχῆς ἀνθρωπίνης δέον προσγενέσθαι
english_gloss: "sowed some into earth, some into moon, some into the other instruments of time, fashion mortal bodies, human soul"
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_2090
feature_label: demiurgos_production_mapping
observation: "After legislating the conditions for mortal souls, the demiurge sows souls into distinct cosmic locations: earth, moon, and the other instruments of time. He then delegates to the younger gods the task of fashioning mortal bodies and completing the remaining human-soul component."
textual_basis: "At 42d-42e the sowing verb distributes souls across three named destinations, and immediately after the sowing the text assigns to the younger gods the work of fashioning mortal bodies, adding what still needed to be added of human soul, and governing the mortal living thing as well as possible."
limits: "This observation records a textual distribution-and-delegation pattern within the mythic narrative. It does not assert that the passage proves a doctrine of astral determinism or that the demiurge's sowing is esoteric."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0117
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 42c-42d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 42c-42d
  start_marker: 42c
  end_marker: 42d
  start_char: 45132
  end_char: 45878
  text_sha256: "255991bc3b701225faca13584d8ca922b91163bd17d32e10151623b5140d11db"
greek_terms:
  - διαθεσμοθετήσας
  - ἀναίτιος
  - τῆς ἔπειτα κακίας
english_gloss: "legislated, not responsible, of subsequent vice"
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1092
feature_label: causal_priority_refutation
observation: "The demiurge's act of legislating all these arrangements is explicitly motivated by a desire to be 'not responsible' for any soul's subsequent vice (42d). This provides a causal-priority argument: the maker establishes the initial conditions and law but the moral failure that triggers the reincarnation cycle belongs to the individual soul, not to the divine cause."
textual_basis: "At 42d the purpose clause states that the legislation ensures the demiurge is not the cause of each soul's subsequent vice. The causal priority is explicit: the initial sowing and law-giving are complete before any soul can become vicious, making the maker causally prior but morally exonerated."
limits: "This observation records an explicit causal attribution and exoneration claim within a mythic passage. It does not claim that this is a hidden proof or that the passage operates as a formal syllogism about divine responsibility."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: ["φύλακα", "δημιουργὸν"]
english_gloss: "guardian and craftsman"
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_3104
feature_label: earth_as_demiourgos
observation: At 40c the earth is given the title of guardian and craftsman of night and day, transferring craft/professional language to a cosmic body.
textual_basis: Timaeus describes the earth as "a guardian and craftsman of night and day" (40c), using the same noun demiourgos that appears elsewhere for divine craftsmanship. The epithet is applied to the earth itself, a generated cosmic body within the heaven.
limits: This observation does not claim that the earth is literally a craftsman; it only registers the transfer of craft vocabulary to a non-human cosmic entity. The passage treats the earth as the first and most venerable among the generated gods, not as a metaphor for human skill.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: ["εἰρημένα", "φύσεως", "ἐχέτω", "τέλος"]
english_gloss: "let what has been said about nature have its end"
feature_family: closure_type
feature_id: feature_candidate_3105
feature_label: explicit_section_close_formula
observation: At the end of 40c-40d the speaker uses a formal closure formula to close the discussion of the visible and generated gods, marking an explicit structural break before turning to the topic of daimons.
textual_basis: The text at 40d states "let what has been said about the visible and generated gods by nature have its end" (eiremena physeos echeto telos). This is a self-conscious structural marker that closes one section of the cosmological account before the transition to "the other daimons."
limits: This observation records a surface textual structure marker, not a claim about the philosophical content of the preceding section. The formula indicates a speaker-controlled division of the discourse, not necessarily a doctrinal closure.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0120

stephanus_span: 40c-40d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 40c-40d
  start_marker: 40c
  end_marker: 40d
  start_char: 41088
  end_char: 42056
  text_sha256: 350e53bc587c7071bb2fa20470e1b931bd5ee6b51cb2b87508d8f3a0f0244bcc
greek_terms: ["δαιμόνων", "ἐκγόνοις", "θεῶν"]
english_gloss: "daimons, descendants of gods"
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_3106
feature_label: genealogy_deferred_to_tradition
observation: At 40d the speaker marks a boundary between the account of the visible generated gods and the account of daimons by declaring that daimon genealogy is beyond human capacity and must be received from earlier traditional authorities who claimed descent from the gods.
textual_basis: The speaker says at 40d that speaking about and knowing the generation of the other daimons is "greater than is in our power" and that "we must trust those who have spoken before, who were descendants of gods, as they said, and presumably knew their own ancestors clearly." This hedges the daimon account behind traditional genealogy rather than cosmological demonstration.
limits: This observation notes a demarcation device, not a claim that Plato rejects the daimon account. The passage explicitly trusts the earlier speakers; the observation only records the structural boundary and the appeal to inherited tradition as the basis for what follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0121
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 42a-42b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 42a-42b
  start_marker: 42a
  end_marker: 42b
  start_char: 44440
  end_char: 45132
  text_sha256: b504052077c8ddc98370eb7bf60fcd2915c60405b5af6e1dc40579077678ac50
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: prosopography
feature_id: feature_candidate_1174
feature_label: ethical_type_profile
observation: At 42a-b the Demiurge's speech enumerates a list of psychic affections that become innate when souls are implanted in bodies: sensation from violent impressions, then eros mixed with pleasure and pain, and in addition fear, spiritedness, and their accompaniments and contraries. Mastery of these yields a life of justice; being mastered yields injustice.
textual_basis: The passage at 42a-b catalogues 'pleasure', 'pain', 'eros', 'fear', 'thumos' and their cognates as innate affections arising from embodiment, then ties the ethical outcome (justice vs. injustice) to whether the soul masters or is mastered by these affections. This maps a typology of character outcomes onto a psychic-affective taxonomy.
limits: This observation records the catalogue of innate affections and the mastery/mastered ethical binary; it does not assess whether this maps onto Republic tripartition or any later psychological theory.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0122
source_work: Timaeus
stephanus_span: 42a-42b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/timaeus.txt
  stephanus_span: 42a-42b
  start_marker: 42a
  end_marker: 42b
  start_char: 44440
  end_char: 45132
  text_sha256: b504052077c8ddc98370eb7bf60fcd2915c60405b5af6e1dc40579077678ac50
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_1188
feature_label: mythic_aetiology
observation: At 42a-b the Demiurge's address provides an aetiology of the difference between male and female embodied natures. The twofold human nature is described, with the stronger kind being that which would later be called 'man' (aner); souls that fail in the mastery of affections are said to pass into a woman's nature in a second birth.
textual_basis: The passage states that human nature is double (diples...tes anthropines phuseos) and the stronger kind would later be called 'aner' at 42a; at 42b it says that one who stumbles (sphaleis) in mastering the affections passes into a woman's nature (eis gunaikos phusin) in the next birth. This provides an aetiological mythic account of sexual differentiation.
limits: The observation notes the aetiological function of the Demiurge's speech regarding sex differentiation and reincarnation; it does not evaluate the content as gender ideology or compare it with other Platonic treatments of gender.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_396
feature_label: reincarnation_by_character_taxonomy
observation: At 42b the Demiurge narrates a reincarnation schema tied to ethical character: one who lives well for the appointed time returns to the habitation of his companion star and enjoys a happy and congenial life; one who fails passes into a woman's nature in the next birth.
textual_basis: The text at 42b states that the one who lives well (eu...bious) for the fitting time returns to the dwelling of his companion star (sunonomou...oikesin astron) and has a blessed and familiar life (bion eudaimona kai sunethe), while the one who stumbles (sphaleis) passes into a woman's nature. This is a reincarnation outcome mapped to ethical desert.
limits: This observation records the reincarnation-by-character taxonomy in the Demiurge's speech; it does not assess its consistency with other Platonic afterlife myths (e.g., Phaedo, Republic X, Phaedrus).
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - αἰσθήσεις
english_gloss: "the six motions ... sense-perceptions"
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_357
feature_label: body_impediment_catalog
observation: At 43b-43c the embodied soul's motions are described as disordered and irrational, moving in all six directions (forward/backward, right/left, up/down), and the flux of incoming bodily affections is explicitly identified as the origin of sense-perceptions (aisthēseis).
textual_basis: The passage catalogs the six directional motions (43b) and then traces how external impacts on the body produce movements that strike the soul, stating that these were therefore called and still are called aisthēseis (43c).
limits: The observation records the naming of sense-perception as a consequence of embodiment; it does not evaluate whether this account is consistent with earlier passages about the soul's composition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 43b the influx of nourishment is described through an image of a flooding and ebbing wave (kataklyzontos kai aporreontos kymatos), and this wave produces even greater disturbance in the soul.
textual_basis: The wave image is directly stated at 43b: 'for the wave that was flooding in and ebbing out, which provided nourishment, produced still greater disturbance.' The disturbance is explictly linked to bodily affections striking the soul.
limits: The observation notes the wave imagery for bodily influx; it does not analyze whether this image is consistent with the earlier construction of the soul from the circle of the Same and the Different.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_3160
feature_label: divine_mixing_graded
observation: The Demiurge's second mixing in the bowl produces soul-stuff that is explicitly graded as "second and third" (deutera kai trita), in contrast to the earlier pure mixture used for the world-soul.
textual_basis: At 41d, after addressing the lesser gods, the Demiurge returns to the mixing bowl (krater) and pours in the remnants of the previous mixture. The text states the mixture is no longer pure in the same way but "second and third" (akerata de ouketi kata tauta hosautos, alla deutera kai trita).
limits: This observes the textual claim of a graded mixture; it does not interpret the metaphysical status of second- and third-grade soul-stuff or its implications for the hierarchy of souls.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 41e, the Demiurge speaks the fated laws (nomous tous heimarmenous) to the souls, declaring that the first birth would be one and the same for all, so that no soul would be disadvantaged by him (genesis prote men esoito tetagmene mia pasin, hina metis elattoito hup' autou).
limits: This observes the egalitarian principle stated in the mythic decree; it does not address what the first birth consists of or how subsequent births differ.

```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 44a-44b, the text states that the soul becomes mindless at first when bound into a mortal body. It then describes how, as the growth-and-nourishment stream lessens and the circles gain calm and stabilize over time, the revolutions are corrected and the soul's possessor becomes intelligent."
limits: "This observation records the developmental claim about the soul's cognitive state upon embodiment within the Timaeus's likely account. It does not include the following alternatives for proper education or neglect, and it does not compare this claim with accounts of soul in other dialogues."

```

```yaml
greek_terms:

  - κατὰ πάντα τελέῳ
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_3168
feature_label: demiurge_looks_to_model
observation: At 30d, Timaeus states that the god wished to make the cosmos like the fairest and most perfect of intelligible things, establishing the demiurge's act of looking to an intelligible model as the explanatory frame for cosmic generation.
textual_basis: Timaeus says the god wished to liken the cosmos to the fairest and most perfect of intelligible things. The verb for wishing ascribes a deliberate intention, and the superlatives fairest and most perfect identify the model as the best within the intelligible domain, setting up the craft analogy that structures the remainder of the cosmogony.
limits: This observation records only the explicit statement that the demiurge looked to an intelligible paradigm. It does not claim that the paradigm is the Form of the Good, nor does it address whether the demiurge is a literal craftsman or a metaphorical figure.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The phrase for single visible living thing directly characterizes the cosmos as a visible entity. The god's act of likening the cosmos to the intelligible paradigm positions the visible world as an image of the intelligible, which is a recurring pattern in Platonic form-language.
limits: This observation does not claim that the passage uses explicit participation vocabulary or that the cosmos is said to be a Form. It notes only the visible/intelligible contrast and the image relation established at 30d.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Critias explains his readiness to retell the story by comparing repeated childhood questioning and instruction to indelible painted marks that have remained in him."
textual_basis: "At 26c, Critias says the things heard as play, with the elder eagerly teaching and the child often asking again, became lasting in him like marks of an unwashable painting; he then says he repeated them to the others that morning."
limits: "This records the memory-authorization frame for the reported story. It does not verify the truth of what is remembered."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  text_sha256: 053ebd03d5277d676b3b54690ff8f8502cc3bc685670be288459b50b36fad9d7
greek_terms: [φιλοσοφίας, ἀγαθὸν, θεῶν, ὄψιν, νοῦ, περιόδους, διανοήσεως]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: teleological_structure
feature_id: feature_candidate_3370
feature_label: vision_given_for_philosophy
observation: "The highest good of the eyes is formulated theologically: the gods gave vision so humans could look at the heavenly revolutions of mind and apply them to the revolutions of human thought."
textual_basis: "At 47b, Timaeus says humans acquired philosophy from sight, calling it the greatest good ever given to mortals by the gods. He then states that god discovered and gave vision so that humans might observe the heavenly periods of mind and use them for the cycles of their own thinking."
limits: "This records the teleological statement about vision and philosophy. It does not identify philosophy with astronomy alone."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: [ἀταράκτοις, τεταραγμένας, λογισμῶν, μιμούμενοι, ἀπλανεῖς, πεπλανημένας, φωνῆς, ἀκοῆς]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: teleological_structure
feature_id: feature_candidate_3371
feature_label: divine_revolutions_correct_human_thought
observation: "The benefit of observing the heavens is corrective imitation: by learning the untroubled divine revolutions and sharing in correct calculation, humans can stabilize the wandering revolutions within themselves; voice and hearing are then assigned the same general purpose."
textual_basis: "At 47c, Timaeus contrasts the untroubled heavenly cycles with the troubled cycles of human thought and says that, by learning and imitating the divine unerring cycles, humans may set right their own wandering ones. He then says the same account applies to voice and hearing, given by the gods for the same ends."
limits: "This records the corrective structure of the teleology. It does not include the more specific role of harmony and rhythm in the next spans."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Harmony is presented as useful not for irrational pleasure but as an ally for bringing the disordered revolution of the soul into order and concord with itself."
textual_basis: "At 47d, Timaeus says what is useful in voice for hearing is given for the sake of harmony. Harmony has motions akin to the soul's inner periods and, for one who uses the Muses with mind, is not for irrational pleasure but for ordering and bringing into concord the disharmonious soul-period within us."
limits: "This records the explicit anti-pleasure and soul-ordering account of harmony. It does not treat all musical pleasure as rejected."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 47e, after the preceding account is marked as mostly complete, Timaeus contrasts things made through intellect with things that come to be through necessity and says the latter must be set alongside in the logos."
limits: "This records the methodological transition only. It does not preserve the preceding rhythm-as-helper clause, and it does not state the following claim that intellect persuades necessity."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: precosmic_element_separation
observation: "Before cosmic ordering, the receptacle's shaking motion separates the four kinds by likeness and unlikeness, so similar things are pushed together and dissimilar things are set apart while everything remains without reason and measure."
textual_basis: "At 53a, Timaeus says the four kinds are shaken by the receptacle as by an instrument that supplies motion; the most unlike are separated from each other, the most alike are driven together, and before the ordered whole comes to be these things are in different places and are without reason and measure."
limits: "This records the pre-cosmic sorting mechanism. It does not identify the four kinds with fully ordered elements yet."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 53b, Timaeus says that when ordering was undertaken, the four kinds had some traces of themselves but were disposed as things are when god is absent; the god first shaped them by forms and numbers and established them as beautifully and excellently as possible from a prior disorderly condition."
limits: "This records the mathematizing craft premise. It does not provide the subsequent geometric construction itself."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 53c, after saying the hearers can follow because they share in the paths of education, Timaeus states that fire, earth, water, and air are bodies; every bodily form has depth, depth necessarily includes plane nature, and a right plane base is composed from triangles."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 53e, Timaeus asks what four most beautiful bodies could be unlike one another yet capable of becoming from one another when dissolved; he says that if they find this they have the truth about the generation of earth, fire, and the middle proportional bodies."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 54a, the isosceles triangle is said to have one nature, while the elongated triangle has unlimited forms; the account therefore chooses the finest among the unlimited candidates, the one from which the equilateral triangle is composed."
limits: "This records the selection rule and its open challenge. It does not supply the longer proof that Timaeus says would be required."

```

```yaml
limits: "This records the structural reason for restricted transformation. It does not yet name the elements assigned to each solid."
```

```yaml
limits: "This records the stated reason for assigning the cube to earth. It does not include the assignments of the remaining elements."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 49b-49c, Timaeus says what is now named water is seen hardening into stones and earth, melting and separating into air, burning into fire, condensing back through air, cloud, and mist into flowing water, and again becoming earth and stones. limits: "This records the transformation sequence as the text presents it. It does not establish a modern physical account or fix the elements as permanent substances."
```

```yaml
observation: "Because the elemental appearances never show themselves as the same, Timaeus says they should not be named as stable items, but only as such-and-such cases like fire or water."
textual_basis: "At 49d, he asks how anyone could confidently assert that any one of these is this and not another when each never appears as the same. He then states the safer way to speak: call what is observed becoming differently not this fire, but what is such as fire, and likewise for water and other cases."
limits: "This records the proposed naming discipline for changing appearances. It does not deny that the appearances occur."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 50d, he says three kinds must be conceived: the becoming thing, the thing in which it becomes, and the source from which the becoming thing grows by likeness. He then compares the receiver to mother, the source to father, and the intermediate nature to offspring, adding that the receiving item must be formless relative to all forms it will receive."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 50e, Timaeus says that if the receiver resembled any entrant, it would receive contrary or wholly different natures badly by showing its own look alongside them. He therefore says what will receive all kinds must be outside all forms, and compares this with making scent-bearing liquids as odorless as possible and preparing soft materials as smooth as possible before stamping shapes into them."
limits: "This records the formless-receiver requirement and its examples. It does not claim the receiver is literally a liquid or craft material."

```

```yaml
limits: "This records the stated contrast between the two cognitive kinds. It does not infer who belongs to the small human group."
```

```yaml
observation: "The previous distinctions are summarized into three: an ungenerated and imperishable form grasped by thought, a sensible generated likeness that comes to be in a place and perishes, and the always-existing kind of space."
textual_basis: "At 52a, Timaeus says one thing has the same form, is ungenerated and imperishable, receives nothing into itself, enters nothing else, and is invisible and insensible, belonging to thought's inspection. A second is homonymous and like it, sensible, generated, always carried, coming to be in some place and perishing from there. A third kind is the always existing space."
limits: "This records the threefold summary. It does not collapse the form, copy, and space into one another."
review_status: accepted
```

Looking toward it, Timaeus says, people dream that every being must be somewhere in a place and occupy some space, and that what is neither on earth nor in heaven is nothing."

```yaml
  assigning the least-mobile remaining form to water, the most-mobile form to

  air between them.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 56d, after marking the account as likely, Timaeus says earth meeting fire is dissolved by fire's sharpness and carried until its parts meet and reassemble with themselves; he explicitly adds that it would never come into another form. The same marker then begins a contrasting account of water divided by fire or air."
limits: "This records earth's restricted transformability and the start of the contrast with water. It does not complete the water conversion ratios, which continue into the next marker."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 56e, Timaeus says pieces of air from one dissolved part would become two fire bodies. He then describes small fire enclosed by air, waters, or earth, defeated in motion, and broken down so that two fire bodies combine into one air form; when air is overcome and divided, two and a half wholes compact into one whole water form."
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_3425
feature_label: elemental_transformation_stability_condition
observation: "Elemental transformation is treated as a contest that stops once the cut material has assembled into the cutter's nature; like does not alter or suffer from like, while a weaker body becoming something else keeps dissolving as long as it fights a stronger one."
textual_basis: "At 57a, Timaeus says a kind caught in fire is cut by the sharpness of fire's angles and sides, but when it has come together into fire's own nature it stops being cut. He explains this with the rule that each like and same kind cannot cause change in, or suffer from, what has the same condition, whereas a weaker thing fighting a stronger one while becoming something else does not stop being dissolved."
limits: "This records the stated stopping condition for transformation. It does not identify every later outcome of the contest."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 57b, Timaeus says small bodies enclosed among many larger ones stop being extinguished when they are willing to assemble into the victor's form, producing air from fire and water from air. If they go into the same place and fight some incoming different kind, dissolution continues until they either flee after being thrust apart and dissolved or are defeated and remain as one thing like the conqueror."
limits: "This records the two possible endpoints of the transformation contest. It does not claim that the text gives a mechanical example for every possible encounter."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 57d, Timaeus says the cause of other kinds being implanted within the elemental forms is that each triangle was not planted at the beginning with only one size, but with smaller and larger sizes in as many numbers as there are kinds within the forms. Because these mix with themselves and with one another, their variety is unlimited, and those who will use a likely account about nature must become observers of it."
limits: "This records the stated source of sub-elemental variety. It does not enumerate the varieties themselves."
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: mathematical_cosmology
feature_id: feature_candidate_3428
feature_label: motion_rest_inequality_principle
observation: "Before proceeding, Timaeus makes motion and rest explicit: motion is said not to occur in uniformity because mover and moved are both required and cannot be uniform, so rest is assigned to uniformity, motion to irregularity, and inequality to the cause of the irregular nature."
textual_basis: "At 57e-58a, Timaeus says the later reasoning needs prior agreement about motion and rest. Motion is not willing to be in uniformity because mover and moved cannot exist apart from each other and cannot ever be uniform; he then places rest in uniformity, motion in irregularity, and names inequality as the cause of the irregular nature."
limits: "This records the motion-rest principle. It does not include the following circular-compression account that explains why the separated kinds keep moving through one another."
review_status: accepted
```

He then names the circular revolution of the whole as enclosing the kinds, tightening everything, and leaving no empty place."
```yaml
limits: "This records only the no-void compression setup at 58a. It does not re-record the rest-in-uniformity and motion-in-irregularity principle, and it does not include the following gap-filling mechanics of small and large bodies."
```

```yaml
limits: "This records the gap-and-compression mechanism within the elemental account. It does not identify the later named subtypes of fire, air, or water."

```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 58d, Timaeus calls the clearest air aether, the muddiest air mist and darkness, and also mentions unnamed forms generated through inequality of triangles. He then divides water into wet and fusible kinds and says the wet kind is moving both by itself and by another because it contains small unequal water kinds and because of irregularity and the form of its shape."
limits: "This records the taxonomy and stated causes of mobility. It does not include the later account of how fire changes the more stationary water kind."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: water_state_change_by_fire_motion
observation: "The heavier, more stationary water kind is said to be fixed by uniformity until incoming fire dissolves it, removes its uniformity, makes it share more in motion, and allows nearby air to push it downward; the resulting affections are named melting and flow."
textual_basis: "At 58e, Timaeus contrasts water made from large uniform bodies with the mobile wet kind, saying the former is more stationary and heavy because it is fixed by uniformity. When fire enters and dissolves it, it loses uniformity and shares more in motion; once mobile, it is pushed by nearby air and stretched toward earth, receiving the names melting for reduction of masses and flow for the downward stretching."
limits: "This records the forward change from fixed water to mobile flow. It does not include the reverse process of cooling and freezing."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elemental_theory
feature_id: feature_candidate_3449
feature_label: water_state_change_by_fire_motion
observation: "The reverse water-state process is defined: when fire departs without entering a void, nearby air pushes the still-mobile wet mass into the seats of fire, mixes it with itself, and lets it regain uniformity; the departure of fire is named cooling, and the coming-together after its departure is named freezing."
textual_basis: "At 59a, Timaeus says that when fire falls out from the wet mass, it does not leave into a void, so nearby air presses the still-mobile wet bulk into the places of fire and mixes it with itself. The mass regains uniformity because fire, described as the maker of irregularity, has gone away; Timaeus names the removal of fire cooling and the post-removal coming-together a frozen kind."
limits: "This records the naming and mechanism of cooling and freezing. It does not cover the subsequent catalogue of fusible waters and metals."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: "At 59b, Timaeus turns to the waters previously called fusible, saying that the kind formed from the finest and smoothest parts, made densest, single-formed, bright, and yellow, is gold hardened after filtering through rock. He then calls a hardest blackened offshoot of gold adamant, and describes a near-gold kind with more than one form that receives a small fine part of earth and becomes harder."
limits: "This records the material taxonomy within the marker. It does not complete the near-gold material's later name or the later account of its corrosion."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

feature_label: material_taxonomy_from_earth_mixtures
observation: "Stone formation is explained through air displacement without void: newly generated air rises, nearby air is pushed, and the heavier surrounding air compresses the earth mass into the seats from which the new air rose. Earth compacted with water in this way becomes rock, with equal, smooth, transparent parts marking the fairer kind and the opposite parts marking the less fair kind."
textual_basis: "At 60c, Timaeus says the generated air rises to its own place, that no void is left over, and that the nearby air is pushed. The pushed heavier air surrounds the earth bulk, presses it hard, and drives it into the places from which the new air rose; earth compressed by air with water becomes rock. The marker then distinguishes the fairer rock by equal, smooth, transparent parts and the opposite kind by contrast."
limits: "This records the rock-generation mechanism and the part-quality contrast within 60c. It does not include the fired-clay and other earth-mixture products that follow."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 60d, Timaeus says the whole moist part is snatched out by the speed of fire and the resulting rougher kind is called ceramic. He also says that when moisture remains, liquid earth cooled after fire becomes a stone with black color. The same marker then describes salty, semi-solid products soluble again by water and names one as cleansing oil and earth."
limits: "This records only the products named or fully described within 60d. It does not complete the second salty product, whose name and sensory setting fall outside this marker."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 60e, Timaeus says common bodies from both elements are not dissolved by water but are compacted so as to be dissolved by fire. He explains that fire and air have parts smaller than the intervals in earth's structure and pass through without force, whereas water's larger parts make a forceful passage and melt by dissolving."
limits: "This records only the first access-based melting rule within 60e. It does not include the salt completion at the start of the marker or the later differentiated rules for compacted earth, water, and air."
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0227

greek_terms: [γῆν, ὕδωρ, λύει, πυρός, εἴσοδος, σύνοδον, διαχεῖτον, διάκενα, τρίγωνα, ἀέρα]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elemental_theory
feature_id: feature_candidate_3461
feature_label: material_solubility_by_elemental_access
observation: "A graded dissolution table is given for earth, water, and air: unconstituted earth under force is dissolved only by water, constituted earth only by fire, the strongest water cohesion only by fire, weaker water cohesion by both fire and air, violently constituted air only at the elemental level, and unforced air only by fire. The marker then begins the case of bodies mixed from earth and water."
textual_basis: "At 61a, Timaeus states separate rules for earth, water, and air. Earth without settled constitution is dissolved only by water, but settled earth admits only fire. The most violent water gathering is dispersed only by fire, while the weaker one is dispersed by fire and air, with different routes through intervals and triangles. For air, nothing dissolves the violently constituted form except according to the element, while unforced air is melted only by fire."
limits: "This records the dissolution rules stated within 61a. It does not complete the account of mixed earth-water bodies, which the marker only begins."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 61b, Timaeus says that as long as water holds the earth intervals compressed by force, water approaching from outside has no entrance, flows around the whole bulk, and leaves it unmelted. Fire enters the intervals of the waters and does to water what water does to earth, so fire alone is the cause of flowing when the common body is melted."
limits: "This records only the access rule for mixed earth-water bodies within 61b. It does not include the list of lower-water mixtures that begins later in the marker."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0217

greek_terms: [παθήματα, αἴσθησιν, σαρκὸς, ψυχῆς, ὑποθετέον, σῶμα]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: method_boundary
feature_id: feature_candidate_3452
feature_label: perceptual_etiology_requires_prior_hypothesis
observation: "After nearly completing the account of bodies varied by shapes, combinations, and transformations, Timaeus shifts to the causes of their affections and marks a methodological dependency: sensation, flesh, the parts around flesh, and the mortal soul are needed for the account, but cannot all be explained at once, so one side must be posited first and returned to later."
textual_basis: "At 61c-61d, Timaeus says the varieties produced by shapes, communions, and changes into one another have almost been displayed, and that the causes of their affections must now be made clear. He adds that sensation must always be present in what is being discussed, while the generation of flesh, surrounding bodily parts, and the mortal part of soul has not yet been treated; because neither the sensory affections nor those bodily and psychic topics can be adequately stated apart from the other, one must be hypothesized first and then revisited."
limits: "This records the explicit ordering problem and ordered method. It does not supply the later full account of flesh or mortal soul."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: perception_generation
feature_id: feature_candidate_3453
feature_label: heat_as_geometric_cutting
observation: "Heat is explained as a sharp bodily affection caused by fire's geometry and motion: thin sides, sharp angles, small particles, and rapid movement make it cut what it meets, divide bodies into small pieces, and thereby give rise to the affection and name now called hot."
textual_basis: "At 61e-62a, Timaeus says everyone nearly senses that the affection is sharp, then appeals to the thinness of sides, sharpness of angles, smallness of parts, and speed of motion. Remembering the generation of fire's shape, he says this nature cuts what it encounters, divides our bodies into small pieces, and plausibly supplies the affection and name hot."
limits: "This records the proposed cause of heat. It does not include the opposite affection of cold, which is treated separately."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_timaeus_0219
source_work: Timaeus
```
